                             EAST LYME FAIR RENT COMMISSION
                                 PUBLIC HEARING MINUTES
                                    JANUARY 29,2025



IN ATTENDANCE: Seated Fair Rent Commission Members Chairman Ste-ve Q3rpenteri,
Deputy Chairman Dan Horgan, John Vilcheck, Nancy lohnson and Mikd.Bek'ech litt memndil
to replace recused member Alicia Chandler). Gwen Dowd (alt member) and Alicia Chandler
(recused from Public Hearing) were also in attendance in the audience_,'.,,..
                                                                                            ,    .
ALSO IN ATTENDANCE: Fair Rent Commission (FRC) Town Staff Member Stacey Konakowitz,
Board of Selectmen Ex-Officio Jason Deeble, Town Attorneys Tracy Collins and Catherine Marrion,
Tenant Representative Attorney Chelsea Connery of the Connecticut Fair Housing Center, and
Alpha Capital representatives Chief Operating Officer Johnny Diaz II and Attorney Richard Pascal.
Mr. Carpenteri called the meeting to order at 7:00 p.m. and led the Pledge of Allegiance.

Attorney Collins explained the protocol for this public hearing, which is a formal proceeding,
stating that comments and/or questions from the public are not allowed. She recommended
that the FRC receive the information tonight from both parties and then schedule a
continuance for the deliberations on another night in order for them to have time to review
everything properly. She stated that the commission members should not speak to each
other or anyone in the public about this; discussion between commission members should
only occur during public deliberations, She introduced Attorney Richard Pascal representing
Alpha Capital LLC, and Attorney Chelsea Connery of the CT Fair Housing Center.

Chairman Carpenteri read the Public Hearing Notice into the record, and then Attorney
Collins administered the oath to testify truthfully to all the witnesses.

For the Tenants
Attorney Chelsea Connery stated that she will have two tenant representatives testify
tonight; these two people were chosen to speak on behalf of all the Windward Village
tenants, Tonya Harris and John Vasko. Attorney Connery reported that she is representing
ten out of the eleven tenants at this public hearing, and that these represented tenants
have lived at Windward Village between three and twenty-two years. It was noted that
most of the tenants overall are elders over the age of 62 and disabled persons. Title 47a of
the General Statues of Connecticut protects both classes at the state level. Attorney
Connery presented documents of evidence, and entered it as Plaintiff's Exhibit A.

Tonya Harris, tenant representative, stated that she was hit by a car in 2OI7 leaving her
disabled. In 202I, she moved into Windward Village which not only offered her a rent that
she could afford on her limited income, but she found that the other residents have become
a large family that help and look out for each other. She stated that before Alpha Capital
purchased the building, everything around the complex was maintained and kept clean, and
now that is not the case. She stated that she has been looking for another place to move
but she cannot find anything affordable, and the housing that is affordable has a four-to-
five-year waitlist.

John Vasko, tenant representative, stated that he has lived at Windward Village for t4
years. His rent was raised from $1,000 to $1,900 per month, which he challenged, and
they ended up at $1,600 per month. He stated that every other tenant that he has spoken
with is in the same position, and his hope is that the commission will come to a conclusion
that is fair and equitable.
For Alpha Capital LLC
Johnny Diaz, Alpha Capital LLC, explained that Alpha Capital is a small corporation based
right here in Connecticut and was started in 2OL7. They purchased their first building in
Bridgeport in 2020, and they now have approximately 364 units throughout Connecticut,
He stated that they have been working with Zoning, specifically to find out if they could turn
Windward Village into affordable senior housing and they were that was not an option for
this property. Since taking ownership, they have completed 286 work orders and spent
approximately $180,000 on the property; and they have future plans to create space for a
book club and to build an outdoor grill area. Upgrades to date include HVAC repairs, carpet
replacement, painting, new appliances and new cabinets in some of the units.

In rebuttal, Tonya Harris and John Vasko both explained that although they did replace
appliances, they replaced full-sized appliances with smaller, much inferior appliances. They
also replaced all of the carpeting, but the former landlord had just done that a couple of
years ago so that was not really necessary.

Staff Member Stacey Konakowitz stated that there is one tenant, Jennifer Benoit, who was
not present at tonight's meeting, and that Jennifer is not represented by the Connecticut
Fair Housing Center.

Attorney Collins stated that the evidentiary portion of the public hearing is now closed, and
the public hearing will be continued to February 4,2025, to open deliberations. She stated
that the commission members should not discuss this matter with anyone from the public or
amongst themselves.

The recording secretary confirmed that all Exhibits have been received and that Record is
complete.

3.    Adjournment
MOTTON (1)
Mr, Horgan MOVED to close the evidentiary portion of the public hearing and adjourn the
January 29,2025, public hearing of the Fair Rent Commission at B:28 p,m.
Seconded by Mr. Vasko. Motion passed 5-0.

Respectfu lly Su bm itted,




Sandra Anderson
Recording Secretary

        We believe that solving the housing crisis requires a partnership between municipalities, private

investors, and the state. Connecticut has resources, such as grants and loans, designed to help create more
housing-both market-rate and affordable. Together, we can make a difference, but it requires all parties
to work toward solutions rather than putting a halt on the free market.


        Alpha Capital is, and has been, committed to bringing affordable housing to the state of
Connecticut and the Town of East Lyme. We have met with the Mayor of New London to discuss housing
initiatives to help Connecticut's residents, we have also reached out to the town of East Lyme on more
than I occasion to discuss affordable housing. At some point, municipalities need to meet our
commitment to senior and/or affordable housing and, in Connecticut, and this great country, putting a halt
on the free market as opposed to adding resources that can create more housing is a destructive solution.
Many residents will state that they will vote against affordable housing to "keep outsiders out," which in
turn hurts the community.


        Our commitment is highlighted by our willingness to hear our residents & creating a significantly
lower renewal rate for our renewing tenants. Our case today is designated to help you understand who
Alpha Capital is, it's designated to dispel the myth that we're anything other than a small Connecticut
company committed to the state and communities we invest in. We make investments that impact our
communities. We're not a Toll Brother's type, or size, organization. We believe, based on the housing
market in East Lyme, that our renewing rental rates are still below market. We believe, based on our
commitment to the building, the maintenance, the market, and our dedication to making sure that
residents have a comfortable place to come home to is what drives our rental rates.


         It's crucial to understand that this situation is not one of a landlord who has owned the building
for years and suddenly decided to spike rents. Rather, it stems from a longstanding pattern where the
previous owner kept rents extraordinarily low, maintaining "yesterday's rents" while selling the property

at "today's market price." This left us, as the new owners, in a position where the rental income no longer
aligned with the financial realities of operating and improving the building.


         When we purchased the building in June 2024, our first priority was to establish trust and

transparency with the tenants. Instead of sending out mass notices announcing sweeping changes, we took

the time to knock on doors, introducing ourselves personally to the residents. We wanted to hear their
 concerns and provide clarity about the changes we were planning.
                                                                                                            6



        We avoided blanket rent increases and instead focused on an individualized approach. For tenants

with expiring leases, we initiated honest conversations about our plans to renovate their units. In cases
where leases were not renewed, we were very flexible in giving tlie residents time to cxplorc other
housing options. However, one of the most common pieces of feedback we received was that they could
not find a more affordable option elsewhere.


        For those who inquired about staying, we were transparent about our plans to adjust rents to
market rates, starting at $1,900 per unit. This honesty allowed tenants to make informed decisions about

whether to stay or seek other arrangements. While some chose to move, others opted to stay and pay the

adjusted rents. As word spread throughout the building, the frustration and concerns from the residents

became clear.


        Throughout this process, we listened carefully to the feedback we received, empathizing with
residents' concems about affordability. We used this input to create a thoughtful plan for renewal
rates-rates that, while still below market, allowed us to effectively manage the property, invest in
necessary improvements, and ensure a high standard of living for all residents.


        This approach highlights our commitment to treating tenants with fairness and respect, while also
addressing the financial realities that come with owning and operating a property at today's costs. Our

goal has always been to strike a balance between maintaining affordability and providing a

well-maintained, high-quality living environment.


        Based on the 13 factors outlined in East Lyme's fair rent ordinance for determining whether a rent

increase is excessive, we will address each one in detail to present a case we strongly stand behind.

                                Reasonable Overhead Expenses

         Operating 468 Main Street, Niantic, comes with substantial financial obligations that must be met

to ensure the property remains functional, safe, and a desirable place to live. These obligations include
essential costs like taxes, insurance, and mortgage payments, which form the foundation of our
operational expenses.


         To provide clarity, the annual taxes on the property are approximately $7,500 per month.

Insurance costs are significant as well, totaling approximately $6,556.67 per month. Our mortgage, which
reflects the financial commitment required to acquire and maintain this property, is approximately
$64,566.25 per month. Together, these three basic expenses alone amount to 578,622'92 per month.


         It's important to note that these fixed costs already exceed the current rent roll for the property,
which as of December 2024 stands at $74,910 per month. This gap highlights the financial challenges
associated with operating the property under current conditions. The previous owner of the property-a

developer who built the building in 1999-owned it free and clear, with no mortgage. This is a significant
distinction, as the lack of debt enabled the previous owner to operate under an entirely different financial
model. For us, as the current owners, these mortgage costs represent a necessary and unavoidable expense
incurred to acquire and improve the properly. (Please see Exhibits A & B, and refer to Exhibit C for an
extensive review of our PAD.

         This limited list of basic expenses does not even account for other necessary operational costs,
 such as maintenance, utilities, staffing, and ongoing renovations-all of which are vital to maintaining a

 high-quality living environment for our residents. Given these realities, it is simply not feasible to sustain
 the property or continue reinvesting in its improvement with rental increases as low as 3o/o or even l0Zo.


         The renewal rental rates we have implemented are still below market value, yet they are
 necessary to support the ongoing operation and improvement of the property. It is also crucial to address

 the broader context: the responsibility for providing affordable housing in the Town of East Lyme should

 not rest solely on the shoulders of small businesses like ours. We contribute taxes to this municipality, and
 we have made every effort to foster a community-oriented approach through our investments and
 improvements. However, the town has consistently denied affordable and senior housing projects that

would alleviate some of this burden.


         It is neither fair nor reasonable to expect a small Connecticut-based company like ours to bear the
financial strain of keeping rental rates artificially low at the expense of operating with negative
profitability. The onus is on the municipality to create an inclusive community by approving projects that
address affordable and senior housing needs. Our rental increases reflect the economic realities of

operating and improving this property, not a disregard for affordability.


         We firmly believe that our approach is both reasonable and necessary to maintain the quality of

life for our residents while ensuring the financial sustainability of the property.

        In order for property owners to maintain exceptionally low rents, they must also have low
operating costs, including minimal or no debt on the property. This underscores the critical need for

municipal and government assistance to support affordable housing initiatives. Without such support,
properties with below-market rents often face significant deferred maintenance. The income generated by

these properties is typically insufficient to cover the necessary reinvestment required to maintain or

upgrade the property adequately.


        When municipalities allow this cycle to persist without intervention, the inevitable outcome is a
tipping point. Over time, the property appreciates in value, and the owner may decide to sell-whether
due to retirement, medical reasons, or other personal circumstances. The new owner, taking on the current

market value of the property, will incur higher expenses, including new debt, insurance premiums, and
operating costs. These increased costs will necessitate raising rental rates to align with the economic
realities of managing the property.
                                                                                                                     9



         If municipalities or governing bodies rule against the ability of new owners to adjust rents to
reflect these increased costs-even when the rates remain below market value-it creates a dangerous
precedent. Such restrictions undermine the equity property owners have spent years building while they
provided a valuable service to the community by keeping rents significantly below market. It effectively
penalizes property owners who have contributed to affordable housing solutions, while failing to address

the systemic issues that led to the affordability crisis in the first place.


        For long-term affordability and sustainability, municipalities must recognize the importance of
proactive support for property owners, whether through subsidies, tax incentives, or grants. By doing so,
they can ensure that low rents are maintained without sacrificing the upkeep of properties or placing
undue financial strain on owners. Failing to do so risks creating a cycle where deferred maintenance and
affordability issues culminate in significant rent increases when ownership changes, exacerbating the very
housing challenges municipalities seek to address.


        If you own a second home and your mortgage is $2,500 per month with taxes of $300 per month,
could you reasonably rent the unit out for less than $2,800 and still meet your financial obligations?
Would you rent it out for $ 1,200 per month simply for the sake of affordability, knowing it would leave
you covering the shortfall out ofpocket?


        Now consider this: if you own a second home and don't have a mortgage on the property, it might
be feasible to allow someone-a sibling, child, family member, or friend-to live there as long as they

pay the taxes and take care of the property. However, that arrangement is only possible because there are

no debt obligations tied to the property, and even then, it's a personal decision that depends on individual

circumstances.


        The same principle applies to apartment buildings, but on a much larger and more complex scale
Just as a homeowner cannot reasonably be expected to rent out their properfy for less than the cost of

maintaining it, a building owner cannot be expected to subsidize rents below operational costs. Unlike a
single home, buildings carry additional layers of expenses                     that increase over time, especially
                                                               -expenses
when a building changes hands at current market prices.
                                                                                                           l0


        If it's not feasible for an individual homeowner to absorb such financial losses, it's even less
feasible for a property owner managing an entire building. To demand otherwise is not only unreasonable
but also unsustainable, and it disregards the economic realitics that come with operating housing in
today's market. This is why municipalities and governments must step in to bridge the gap, rather than
placing the entire burden on private property owners who are already facing rising costs and increased

regulatory pressures.

        The fundamental principle underpinning state and federal housing policy is that operating a
property at very-low-income or low-income rental rates is unsustainable without significant government
support. This principle is codified in a variety of federal and state programs that offer financial support,
tax breaks, and subsidies to landlords and developers who agree to maintain units at affordable rates.
These programs exist precisely because policymakers understand the economic realities of

                                                  Final Statement

           In conclusion, our request for a rent increase is not only fair but also necessary to sustain and
enhance the operations of 468 Main Street. Our portfolio demonstrates a stronger commitment to

affordable housing than the Town of East Lyme, where over l4o/o of our rental units are subsidized. In
contrast, as of 2021, East Lyme's affordable housing units represented just 6.1% of its housing stock,
falling significantly short of Connecticut's statewide goal of 10%. This disparity underscores our
proactive approach to addressing housing needs, which is further evidenced by our repeated outreach to

municipal and state representatives to collaborate on affordable and senior housing initiatives.
Unfortunately, our efforts have been met with resistance, cancellations, and inaction, highlighting a lack
of commitment flom the town to foster an inclusive community.

           Connecticut State Statute 8-30g, enacted in 1989, established a goal that l}Yo ofeach
municipality's housing stock quali$r as "affordable" housing. If a municipality fails to meet this target,
developers may propose projects that are not subject to local zoning regulations. While this statute
provides us with the ability to appeal local zoning to pursue affordable housing projects, we have

consistently chosen to engage with the town to ensure that any developments align with the interests of

taxpayers, elected officials, and municipal leaders. Our goal has always been to work collaboratively and
in sync with the community, despite the challenges posed.


In addition, we currently have 9 new tenants who are paying market-rate rents, along with 9 tenants who
resigned their leases at our proposed increases, which serves as clear proofofconcept and validates the

strong market demand for housing at these rates. These residents willingly entered into leases at the
adjusted prices, demonstrating that the value we offer-through upgraded living spaces, improved

amenities, and high-quality service-meets the expectations of the current rental market.


           Our commitment to providing quality housing is evident in our reinvestment into the property. We
have already spent over $ 180,000 on meaningful upgrades. These investments, current and future, include

cosmetic updates, improved amenities such as on-site laundry and gym facilities, repairs to the HVAC
system, and spaces for community engagement, such as book clubs. Every improvement is reviewed and

approved by the East Lyme Building Department, demonstrating our dedication to compliance and
quality.


           Moreover, the operational costs for this property are substantial. Basic expenses such as taxes,
insurance, and our mortgage exceed $78,600 monthly, surpassing the current rent roll of $74,910, as of
                                                                                                            20


December 2024.These figures do not even account for additional costs like maintenance, utilities, and
staffing. It is critical to understand that the previous owner operated without a mortgage, allowing them to
sustain lower costs. However, we acquired this property with debt, which represents a neccssary and
unavoidable financial reality. Expecting us to shoulder these costs without appropriate rental adjustments
creates an unreasonable burden andjeopardizes the financial sustainability ofthe property.


        Our commitment to high-quality service for our residents is evident in the 286 maintenance work
orders completed since June 2024,bhe $18,000+ spent on labor, and the approximately $5,779 on
materials-all resulting in maintenance satisfaction ratings averaging greater than 4 out of 5. We take
pride in providing an exceptional living experience that exceeds what was previously available to
residents. This level ofservice requires resources, which necessitates fair and reasonable rental rates.


        As Mayor Passero and his team in New London have recognized, increasing housing stock leads

to downward pressure on rents. East Lyme would benefit from adopting a similar forward-thinking
approach. Forcing small businesses like ours to operate at a loss through unreasonable rental caps sets a
dangerous precedent, eroding the equity that landlords have spent years building and stalling

much-needed housing progress.


        Ultimately, we agree with the Town of East Lyme's Affordable Housing Plan, which clearly
outlines that operating multi-family housing at low-income rental rates is not financially feasible without
municipal or government assistance. By capping rental rates without providing sufficient support, the
current approach directly contradicts the conclusions of the town's own assessment.


        The Affordable Housing Plan emphasizes that the financial feasibility of housing developments

depends on a balance between costs and returns. It states, "soft- to weak-market conditions and

marginally feasible developments often require public participation in the firrm of subsidieso the
most common being tax abatements." Furthermore, the plan acknowledges that "below-market rents"
create additional risks and lower returns, making it challenging to operate affordable housing without

support. Most notably in the Affordable Housing Plan, the plan does not recommend capping rental rates

as a strategy to achieve affordable housing. Instead, it suggests alternative measures.




        By enforcing rent caps, landlords are being asked to bear the financial burden ofproviding
affordable housing without the necessary assistance to offset costs. This undermines the plan's
